Causal decompositions of one-dimensional quantum cellular automata
Augustin Vanrietvelde, Octave Mestoudjian, Pablo Arrighi

Abstract
Understanding quantum theory’s causal structure stands out as a major matter, since it radically departs from classical notions of causality. We present advances in the research program of causal decompositions , which investigates the existence of an equivalence between the causal and the compositional structures of unitary channels. Our results concern one-dimensional Quantum Cellular Automata (1D QCAs), i.e. unitary channels over a line of N N quantum systems (with or without periodic boundary conditions) that feature a causality radius r r : a given input cannot causally influence outputs at a distance more than r r . We prove that, for N ≥ 4 ​ r + 1 N\geq 4r+1 , 1D QCAs all admit causal decompositions: a unitary channel is a 1D QCA if and only if it can be decomposed into a unitary routed circuit of nearest-neighbour interactions, in which its causal structure is compositionally obvious. This provides the first constructive form of 1D QCAs with causality radius one or more, fully elucidating their structure. In addition, we show that this decomposition can be taken to be translation-invariant for the case of translation-invariant QCAs. Our proof of these results makes use of innovative algebraic techniques, leveraging a new framework for capturing partitions into non-factor sub-C* algebras.



1
Introduction

Quantum theory’s
causal structure
stands out as one of its most defining features, dramatically encapsulating its departure from classical notions. While the violation of Bell inequalities demonstrates the inadequacy of classical causal modelling in a quantum context, unitary quantum theory is endowed with its own, very robust, notion of causal structure, as articulated e.g. by the framework of quantum causal models
[

2

3

(2)

This definition can be readily extended to channels with arbitrary numbers of inputs and outputs.

A promising direction for better understanding the causal structure of unitary quantum theory is to explore its ties with a closely related but a priori distinct notion — with which it is, in fact, routinely confused — that of its
compositional structure
. A statement about our previous
\mathcal{U}
’s compositional structure would, for instance, be that it can be built out of unitary channels
\mathcal{U}_{1}
and
\mathcal{U}_{2}
in the following way:

(3)

To better express the respective significance of causal and compositional structures, it is enlightening to emphasise their different conceptual foundations. In broad terms, the absence of causal influence, expressed in (
2
) is an
operational
statement, which can for instance be framed in terms of the possible signalling between agents related by the unitary channel
\mathcal{U}
. The decomposition into local operators, expressed in (
3
), on the other hand, has no obvious operational meaning; rather, it concerns our ability to fine-grain the dynamics at hand, with the identification of a causal mediator
X
. What (
3
) lacks in operational meaning compared to (
2
), it gains in mathematical intelligibility, telling us valuable information about the inner structure of
\mathcal{U}
and the ways in which it can be built from elementary unitary channels. Since the decomposition (
3
) captures all and only those channels obeying the causal influence constraint, we call it a
causal decomposition
.

Indeed in (finite-dimensional) unitary quantum theory, (
2
) and (
3
) are equivalent
[
4
]
. This most quintessential causal decomposition has two major consequences. First, the two conceptually distinct notions of causal and compositional structures turn out — at least for the simple case at hand — to be two faces of the same coin. Second, it unlocks a
constructive form
of the unitary channels satisfying the no-influence relation (
2
): they are all and only the channels that can be built in the form (
3
).

A natural research direction, put forward in Ref.
[
5
]
, is then to investigate whether the above equivalence generalises to the case of other, more involved causal structures, relating more input and more outputs. Ref.
[
5
]
showed that all causal structures relating three inputs to three outputs indeed admit such causal decompositions (some of which have to be written in the framework of unitary routed circuits
[
6
,
7
]
, an extension of standard unitary quantum circuits). That every causal structure is equivalent to a corresponding causal decomposition in unitary quantum theory remains, to this date, an open conjecture.
1
1

1

One might also wonder whether this holds for the theory of quantum channels. While this is indeed the case for simple instances — Ref.
[
4
]
’s result in fact applies to channels in general, and Ref.
[
8
]
proves a causal decomposition for quantum channels that satisfy the ‘W’ causal structure — this cannot be the case in general, due to the fact that quantum channels feature no causal structure but a mere
signalling
structure, that does not satisfy the important property of
atomicity

[
3
]
: a quantum channel may exhibit no signalling from
A
to
B
and from
A
to
C
, while exhibiting signalling from
A
to the joint system
BC
. Such a signalling structure cannot find any compositional counterpart in terms of absences of paths in a circuit. Thus, a decomposition encoding all of the information present in a given signalling structure cannot be provided in general for quantum channels.
Proving it would unveil a remarkable correspondence between the informational and the structural aspects of the theory.

Quantum Cellular Automata
(QCAs)
[
9
,
10
]
form an important class of quantum dynamics singled out by their causal structure (see the reviews
[
11
,
12
]
). In the most general definition, a QCA consists of a unitary dynamics defined on a given graph of quantum systems (or sites), and displaying, with respect to this graph’s notion of distance, a certain causality radius
r
: a given input site cannot causally influence output systems at a distance
>r
from it.
2
2

2

A condition of homogeneity is also usually imposed, but as we shall see, it is not important for this paper’s results.
QCAs have been proposed as a model of distributed quantum computing
[
13
,
14
]
, and as models of discretised spacetimes, with the causality radius enforcing relativistic causality, e.g. in order to cast quantum simulation algorithms for quantum field theory
[
15
,
16
,
17
,
18
]
.

A question of crucial importance to better understand QCAs is that of their classification: what are the possible ones? Of equal importance is that of their construction: how to build them? Answering these questions means fully figuring out (in a simplified, discretised case) the exact scope of quantum dynamics consistent with the causal structure of a Minkowski spacetime. The most satisfactory result, solving both problems at once, would be to obtain a
constructive form
analogous to (
3
): a way to specify QCAs as all and only those
\mathcal{U}
’s that can be built in a certain way from elementary unitary channels.

So far, a fully constructive form has only been found for QCAs of radius
\frac{1}{2}
over a 1D lattice (i.e. a line): they are all and only the unitary channels of the form

{

{

{}{}{}}

{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 98.43 -49.21 L 98.43 -34.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -98.43 -49.21 L -98.43 -34.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}{}{}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -49.21 -49.21 L -49.21 -34.45}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{

(4)

where each of the smaller boxes is a unitary channel
[
10
,
19
,
20
]
. We can precisely recognise this, in hindsight, as an instance of a causal decomposition. However, even in 1D, the existence of causal decompositions (or of any constructive form) had so far not been proven for radii greater than
\frac{1}{2}
(see the discussion of previous results at the end of this Section).

In this paper, we prove the existence of causal decompositions for 1D QCAs, in the case of finite length segments or finite diameter loops. Namely, we show that for any
r
, any 1D QCA of radius
r
over a lattice of length strictly greater than
4r
, admits a causal decomposition generalising (
4
). For instance, our result yields that 1D QCAs of radius
1
are all and only the unitary operators of the form

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\,,

(5)

while higher radii are simply given by stacking up more layers (see Figure
1
for the general form). In the case of a translation-invariant QCA, we prove that the decomposition itself can be written in a translation-invariant way.

One should note that such decompositions cannot be expressed in terms of standard unitary quantum circuits; as is (
5
), they are written in terms of the more general
unitary routed circuits
, a more flexible, fully formalised framework aimed at better capturing representations of non-factor C* algebras
[
6
,
7
]
. These feature distinctive floating labels
i,j,\ldots
, which serve to denote the use of blends of direct sums and tensor products when combining Hilbert spaces. For sufficiently complex causal structures, causal decompositions do not exist in standard unitary circuits without routes (see Ref.
[
5
]
for a counterexample).

We thus have a fully constructive form, and one of dramatic physical significance: in discretised 1D, a unitary dynamics is causal if and only if it can be described as a sequence of nearest-neighbour interactions. This also yields a full understanding of the structure of 1D QCAs, of any radius.

Our result is also noteworthy for its novel proof technique, which relies on the introduction of a (concurrently developed) theory of partitions of (finite-dimensional) C* algebras into an arbitrary number of subsystems that, crucially, account for the latter’s potential non-factorness
[
21
]
. Our proofs unveil many interesting structural features of the partitions involved in the decompositions, in particular the interplay between algebras’ centres and the 1D lattice’s metric and topological structures. We expect the insights provided by this innovative structural perspective to prove useful for future extensions of these results.

Before closing this introduction with a discussion of our results’ relationships with previous work, let us present this paper’s structure. We start with an introduction to the theory of partitions and a definition of QCAs over them, generalising the standard QCAs defined over factorisations (Section
2
). We then prove our main result in the C* algebraic picture (Section
3
), before showing how, through the use of routed quantum circuits, it can be expressed in terms of (routed) maps between Hilbert spaces as well (Section
4
). We conclude in Section
5
.

Relationship with previous work

A certain number of results have been found that partly elucidate the structure of QCAs. In particular, Ref.
[
10
]
proved that 1D QCAs of radius
\frac{1}{2}
are all and only the unitaries of the form (
4
). A source of confusion is that it was claimed in Ref.
[
10
]
that the same result also held for spatial dimensions
2
and more; but the proof in those cases turned out to be broken. The proof was curated for dimension
1
in Ref.
[
19
]
, where a counterexample was also given for higher-dimension. The same ideas lead to the theory of a 1D QCA’s index
[
20
]
.

Because a 1D QCA of arbitrary causality radius
r
can always be coarse-grained (by grouping sites) into a 1D QCA of radius
\frac{1}{2}
, all 1D QCAs can be classified by their index, and put into the circuit form (
4
). However, resorting to coarse-graining then applying the radius
\frac{1}{2}
decomposition fails to provide a causal decomposition, or a constructive form, for QCAs of radius
r
: it yields a decomposition that corresponds to a causality radius
2r-\frac{1}{2}
instead. This failure translates into a practical obstacle to constructing QCAs of radius
r
: using this circuit form, one will also produce QCAs of larger radius.

Another important structural result is that QCAs over arbitrary graphs, and with an arbitrary causality radius, can all be implemented by a local circuit of finite depth
[
22
]
. Albeit appealing for its robustness and generality, this result again fails to provide a causal decomposition, for two reasons. First, the unitaries used in such a decomposition also act on ancillary systems, with the promise that the total action amounts to an identity on these ancillary systems, something that cannot be checked easily when starting from an arbitrary decomposition of that form. Second, these unitaries are also promised to commute pairwise, a condition which, here again, cannot be readily checked. Clearly, these two departures from fully constructive form lead to practical obstacles towards constructing QCAs of a given radius.

Figure 1
:
Causal decomposition of a 1D QCA of radius
r
.
r
is either an integer or a half-integer. (In the latter case, the decomposition would end with shifted outputs
B_{\frac{1}{2}},B_{\frac{3}{2}},\ldots
) This uses the language of routed unitary quantum circuits, and more specifically here, of index-matching circuits
[
6
]
. All boxes represent routed unitaries.

Theorem
4.2
states that a unitary over a (possibly looped) 1D graph of
N
finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces, with
N>4r
, is a 1D QCA of radius
r
if and only if it admits a decomposition of this form. Theorem
4.3
states that it is a translation-invariant such QCA if and only if it admits a decomposition of this form in which all boxes of a same layer are identical.

2
Partitions and QCAs over them

The most important ingredient of this paper — essential to the statement and proof of its main result — is the theory of
partitions
of a quantum system. These generalise factorisations in order to accommodate the potential non-factorness of the C* algebras corresponding to some parts of the system. Here, we provide basic definitions and facts: further discussions, as well as proofs of the theorems, can be found in Ref.
[
21
]
.

2.1
Reminders about C*-algebras

Partitions leverage the theory and structure of C* algebras; let us remind the basics. All our further definitions and theorems pertain solely to finite dimension (and for the sake of brevity, we won’t recall it every time).
3
3

3

In the context of this paper, we could equivalently use
Von Neumann algebras
, which in finite dimension are the same thing as C* algebras.

Definition 2.1

(C* algebras)
.

A (finite-dimensional)
* algebra

{{{\omega}}}
is a (finite-dimensional) algebra over complex numbers, equipped with a dagger, i.e. an antilinear involution satisfying
(fg)^{\dagger}=g^{\dagger}f^{\dagger}
.

A
sub-* algebra

\mathcal{A}
of
{{{\omega}}}
is a (unital) subalgebra of
{{{\omega}}}
, closed under the dagger. Given two sub-* algebras
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
we denote by
\mathcal{A}_{1}\vee\mathcal{A}_{2}
their algebraic and
\dagger
closure, which is also a sub-* algebra.

We say that
{{{\omega}}}
is a
C* algebra
if it is embeddable as a sub-* algebra of some space of complex matrices, equipped with the standard adjoint as its dagger. Any sub-* algebra of
{{{\omega}}}
is then a C* algebra as well, and can thus also be called a sub-C* algebra of
{{{\omega}}}
.

In finite dimension, typical examples of C* algebras are spaces
\Lin(\mathcal{H})
of linear operators over a certain finite-dimensional Hilbert space. If
{{{\omega}}}\cong\Lin(\mathcal{H}_{\Omega})
for a certain
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
, we call
{{{\omega}}}
a
factor
. Not all C* algebras are factors, however; the general case is for them to be a direct sum of factors. To understand how this plays out, we need the notion of a centre.

Definition 2.2

(Centre)
.

The centre of a C* algebra
{{{\omega}}}
is

\mathcal{Z}({{{\omega}}}):=\{f\in{{{\omega}}}\,|\,\forall g\in{{{\omega}}},fg=gf\}\,.

(6)

The centre of a C* algebra is a commutative C* algebra, and as such satisfies the following theorem.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}
be a commutative C* algebra. Then there exists a unique finite set
\textrm{AtomProj}(\mathcal{Z})=\{\pi^{k}\}_{k\in K}
of non-zero projectors of
\mathcal{Z}
, called
\mathcal{Z}
’s atomic projectors, satisfying
\pi^{k}=(\pi^{k})^{\dagger}
,
\pi^{k}\pi^{l}=\delta^{kl}\pi^{k}
and
\sum_{k}\pi^{k}=\mathbb{1}
, and forming a basis of
\mathcal{Z}
.

Centres are crucial tools to deal with C* algebras. In particular, the non-factorness of
{{{\omega}}}
is witnessed by the existence of a non-trivial centre (i.e. one not reduced to scalar multiples of the identity
\mathbb{1}
), and this centre’s atomic projectors then precisely designate each of
{{{\omega}}}
’s summand factors.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
{{{\omega}}}
be a C* algebra. We denote its centre’s atomic projectors as
\textrm{AtomProj}(\mathcal{Z}({{{\omega}}}))=\{\pi^{k}\}_{k\in K}
. Then each of the
\pi^{k}{{{\omega}}}:=\{\pi^{k}f=f\pi^{k}|f\in{{{\omega}}}\}
is a factor, i.e.
\pi^{k}{{{\omega}}}\cong\Lin(\mathbb{C}^{d_{k}})
and

\begin{split}{{{\omega}}}&=\biguplus_{k}\pi^{k}{{{\omega}}}\\
&\cong\bigoplus_{k}\pi^{k}{{{\omega}}}\\
&\cong\bigoplus_{k}\Lin(\mathbb{C}^{d_{k}})\,,\end{split}

(7)

where the
\biguplus
denotes the linear span of the union. Consequently,
{{{\omega}}}
is a factor if and only if
\textrm{AtomProj}(\mathcal{Z}({{{\omega}}}))=\{\mathbb{1}\}
.

Thus, any non-factor C* algebra can concretely be seen as (i.e. is isomorphic to) an algebra of block-diagonal matrices; its centre’s atomic projectors are the projectors onto each of its blocks.

Finally, let us introduce commutants, which are a convenient way of defining a sub-C* algebra’s complement.

Definition 2.3

(Commutant)
.

Let
{{{\omega}}}
be a C* algebra and
\mathcal{A}
a sub-C* algebra of it.
\mathcal{A}
’s
commutant
within
{{{\omega}}}
is

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}:=\{f\in{{{\omega}}}\,|\,\forall g\in{{{\omega}}},fg=gf\}\,.

(8)

If
{{{\omega}}}
is a factor, then
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}=\mathcal{A}
(see Proposition
B.2
), which also implies that
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}):=\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=:\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
.

2.2
Partitions

We recall the salient aspects of the theory of partitions, introduced and motivated in detail in Ref.
[
21
]
. To alleviate notations, from now on we write
\mathcal{Z}_{n}:=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}_{n})
.

Definition 2.4

(Bipartitions)
.

Let
{{{\omega}}}
be a C*-algebra, and
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2}\subseteq{{{\omega}}}
sub-C* algebras of it.
We say that
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
forms a
bipartition
of
{{{\omega}}}
(denoted
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})\vdash{{{\omega}}}
) if the following two conditions are satisfied:

\forall\pi\in\textrm{AtomProj}(\mathcal{Z}({{{\omega}}})),\,\,\pi\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\prime}=\pi\mathcal{A}_{2}\,;

(9)

\mathcal{Z}({{{\omega}}})\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{1}\vee\mathcal{Z}_{2}\,.

(10)

If
{{{\omega}}}
is a factor, then (
10
) is trivially satisfied and (
9
) reduces to the standard condition that the algebras are each other’s commutant,

\mathcal{A}_{1}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}_{2}\,.

(11)

In the general case, if
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})\vdash{{{\omega}}}
then
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
commute, but they are not necessarily each other’s commutant (see Proposition
B.4
in the Appendix).

A striking and very important fact (which we call ‘failure of local tomography’, or FOLT) is that in general, the algebraic span of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
, which we denote as
\mathcal{A}_{1}\vee\mathcal{A}_{2}
, does
not
give back the algebra that they bipartition (one can refer to Proposition
B.6
for a characterisation of cases in which it does).

Partitions into more than two parts are obtained by leveraging the definition of bipartitions. Note that, due to FOLT, a partition has to be specified by associating a sub-C* algebra to every subset of the set indexing the partition, and not just to every element of it. (We denote
\mathcal{P}(X)
for the powerset of
X
and
\textrm{Sub}\left({{{\omega}}}\right)
for the set of sub-C* algebras of
{{{\omega}}}
.)

Definition 2.5

(Partitions)
.

Let
{{{\omega}}}
be a C* algebra. A
partition
of it, labelled by the finite set
X
, is a mapping

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}:\,\,\mathcal{P}(X)&\to\textrm{Sub}\left({{{\omega}}}\right)\\
S&\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{S}\,,\end{split}

(12)

satisfying the following conditions:

\mathcal{A}_{X}={{{\omega}}}\,;

(13)

\mathcal{A}_{\emptyset}=\{\lambda\mathbb{1}\,|\,\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\}\,;

(14)

\begin{split}&\forall S,T\subseteq X\textrm{, disjoint, }(\mathcal{A}_{S},\mathcal{A}_{T})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{S\sqcup T}\,.\end{split}

(15)

We then denote
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq X}\vdash{{{\omega}}}
.

Factorisations, i.e. partitions into factors, appear as a special case.

Example 1

(Factorisations)
.

A
factorisation
is a special case of a partition of a factor C*-algebra
{{{\omega}}}
, specified by the data of a family of factor sub-C* algebras of it
(\mathcal{A}_{n})_{n\in X}
, pairwise commuting and satisfying

\bigvee_{n\in X}\mathcal{A}_{n}={{{\omega}}}\,.

(16)

In a factorisation, the conjoined algebras are given by

\forall S\subseteq X,\quad\mathcal{A}_{S}:=\bigvee_{n\in S}\mathcal{A}_{n}\,.

(17)

2.3
1D QCAs over partitions

From now on we restrict to the case of partitions over a 1D graph. By this, we mean that the partition’s set of indices
X
is (the set of vertices of) a graph
\Gamma
that is connected and in which every vertex has at most two neighbours. As we also restricted to
\Gamma
finite, there are only two possibilities: either
\Gamma
is a segment, or it is a loop. As any partition and any QCA in the former case can easily be embedded into the latter, we will concentrate on loops. Denoting
N
as the graph’s size, a natural way to label the vertices is then via elements of the cyclic group
\Gamma_{N}:=\mathbb{Z}_{N}
of integers modulo
N
.

To help intuition, we introduce a notation in which a given 1D partition is represented as a set of wires, each corresponding to one of the parts:

\mathcal{A}_{\shortm 2}
\mathcal{A}_{\shortm 1}
\mathcal{A}_{0}
\mathcal{A}_{1}
\mathcal{A}_{2}

(18)

Note that this is just an informal graphical language; a formal one, based on routed circuits, will be introduced in Section
4
.

To be able to define QCAs of half-integer radius, it is convenient to label the inputs and outputs differently, by also using
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}:=\{n+\frac{1}{2}\,\,\textrm{ mod }N\,|\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
(see Figure
2
).
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
is just another way of writing
\Gamma_{N}
, by shifting its labels by
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
.

0
\shortm 1
\shortm 2
1
2
\shortm\frac{9}{4}
\shortm\frac{7}{4}
\shortm\frac{5}{4}
\shortm\frac{3}{4}
\shortm\frac{1}{4}
\frac{1}{4}
\frac{3}{4}
\frac{5}{4}
\frac{7}{4}
\frac{9}{4}
\shortm\frac{1}{2}
\shortm\frac{3}{2}
\shortm\frac{5}{2}
\frac{1}{2}
\frac{3}{2}
\frac{5}{2}
\Gamma_{N}
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}

Figure 2
:
\Gamma_{N}
,
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
, and
\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}
.

We also need to introduce a notion of distance. For a given
N
, and
0\leq m,n<N
, we denote
d(m,n)=\min(\absolutevalue{n - m},N-\absolutevalue{n - m})
, the distance between
m
and
n
modulo
N
. For a 1D graph
\Gamma
indexed with elements of
[0,N[
,
S\subseteq\Gamma
, and
l>0
, we denote
S\pm l:=\{m\in\Gamma\,|\,\exists n\in S,d(m,n)\leq l\}
.

Definition 2.6

(Generalised 1D quantum cellular automaton)
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
r
a positive integer or half-integer. We denote
\Gamma^{\textrm{out}}:=\Gamma_{N}
when
r
is integer and
\Gamma^{\textrm{out}}:=\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
when it is half-integer. Let
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}}\vdash{{{\omega}}}^{\textrm{in}}
,
(\mathcal{B}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma^{\textrm{out}}}\vdash{{{\omega}}}^{\textrm{out}}
be partitions of two (finite-dimensional) factor C* algebras.

A
quantum cellular automaton
of causality radius
r
from the former to the latter is a C* algebra isomorphism
\mathcal{U}:{{{\omega}}}^{\textrm{in}}\to{{{\omega}}}^{\textrm{out}}
satisfying

\forall S\subseteq\Gamma_{N},\quad\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{S})\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{S\pm r}\,.

(19)

We recover standard QCAs as a special case.
4
4

4

In fact, QCAs are usually introduced as being even more specific than this: they are defined on factorisations in which the factors all are isomorphic, and assumed to be translation-invariant. We will get back to this in Section
3.2
.

Example 2

(1D QCAs on factorisations)
.

A specific example of a 1D QCA is when the input and output partitions are factorisations (as defined in Example
1
). (
19
) can then be replaced with

\forall n\in\Gamma,\quad\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{n})\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\{n\}\pm r}\,.

(20)

The following proposition generalises the invertibility of QCAs, proven in Ref.
[
22
]
for QCAs on factorisations, to our setting. Its proof is presented in Appendix
B.5
.

Proposition 2.1
.

If
\mathcal{U}
is a QCA of causality radius
r
, then so is
\mathcal{U}^{-1}
.

Building on the representation (
18
) of 1D partitions, we can represent 1D QCAs informally as

\mathcal{U}
\mathcal{A}_{\shortm 2}
\mathcal{A}_{\shortm 1}
\mathcal{A}_{0}
\mathcal{A}_{1}
\mathcal{A}_{2}
\mathcal{B}_{\shortm 2}
\mathcal{B}_{\shortm 1}
\mathcal{B}_{0}
\mathcal{B}_{1}
\mathcal{B}_{2}

(21)

or

\mathcal{U}
\mathcal{A}_{\shortm 2}
\mathcal{A}_{\shortm 1}
\mathcal{A}_{0}
\mathcal{A}_{1}
\mathcal{A}_{2}
\mathcal{B}_{\shortm\frac{3}{2}}
\mathcal{B}_{\shortm\frac{1}{2}}
\mathcal{B}_{\frac{1}{2}}
\mathcal{B}_{\frac{3}{2}}

(22)

depending on whether
r
is a half-integer.

3
Causal decompositions of 1D QCAs

3.1
The general case

We define
\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}:=\{\frac{2n+1}{4}\,\,\textrm{ mod }N\,|\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\}=\{\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4},\ldots,N-\frac{1}{4}\}
(See Figure
2
). Its purpose is to index ‘fine-grainings’ of partitions over
\Gamma_{N}
or
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\Gamma
be either
\Gamma_{N}
or
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
, and
(\mathcal{A}_{S}^{\textrm{in}})_{S\subseteq\Gamma}\vdash{{{\omega}}}^{\textrm{in}}
,
(\mathcal{A}_{S}^{\textrm{out}})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}}\vdash{{{\omega}}}^{\textrm{out}}
be connected partitions of factors. A
fine-graining
from the former to the latter is an isomorphism of C* algebras
\mathcal{U}^{\textrm{fine}}:{{{\omega}}}^{\textrm{in}}\to{{{\omega}}}^{\textrm{out}}
satisfying

\forall S\subseteq\Gamma,\quad\mathcal{U}^{\textrm{fine}}(\mathcal{A}_{S}^{\textrm{in}})=\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{out}}_{S\pm\frac{1}{4}}\,.

(23)

A
coarse-graining
is the inverse of a fine-graining.

In our informal graphical notation, we can denote a fine-graining of a partition over
\Gamma_{N}
as

\shortm 2
\shortm 1
0
1
2
\shortm\frac{1}{4}
\frac{1}{4}
\shortm\frac{3}{4}
\shortm\frac{5}{4}
\shortm\frac{7}{4}
\shortm\frac{9}{4}
\frac{5}{4}
\frac{3}{4}
\frac{9}{4}
\frac{7}{4}

(24)

and a coarse-graining symmetrically as

\shortm 2
\shortm 1
0
1
2
\shortm\frac{1}{4}
\frac{1}{4}
\shortm\frac{3}{4}
\shortm\frac{5}{4}
\shortm\frac{7}{4}
\shortm\frac{9}{4}
\frac{5}{4}
\frac{3}{4}
\frac{9}{4}
\frac{7}{4}

(25)

while those over
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
take a symmetric form. In this graphical notation, performing a
\Gamma_{N}
-fine-graining then a
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
-coarse-graining looks like

\shortm 2
\shortm 1
0

\shortm\frac{3}{2}
\shortm\frac{1}{2}
\frac{1}{2}
\frac{3}{2}

(26)

in which we can intuitively see that this yields a QCA of radius
\frac{1}{2}
(which is indeed straightforward to prove formally). This yields the tools to express this paper’s main theorem: 1D QCAs are decomposable as sequences of such radius-
\frac{1}{2}
QCAs.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a 1D QCA between factorisations over
\Gamma_{N}
or
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
, with causality radius
r
, where
N>4r
.

There exist
2r
(generalised) 1D QCAs
\mathcal{U}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{2r}
, with causality radius
\frac{1}{2}
, such that

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{2r}\ldots\mathcal{U}_{1}\,.

(27)

Furthermore, in that case, each of the
\mathcal{U}_{i}
’s decomposes as

\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\textrm{coarse}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\textrm{fine}}\,,

(28)

where
\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\textrm{fine}}
is a fine-graining and
\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\textrm{coarse}}
is a coarse-graining.

Note that in the previous theorem, the
\mathcal{U}_{i}
’s alternately map from a partition over
\Gamma_{N}
to a partition over
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
and back. Each time, this is done by fine-graining the partition over (say)
\Gamma_{N}
to one over
\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}
, then coarse-graining the previous to a partition over
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
. This becomes clearer using our graphical notation, in which the decomposition for an integer
r
becomes

\shortm 2
\shortm 1
0
1
2
\shortm 2
\shortm 1
0
1
2

(29)

with a similar form for
r
a half-integer.

The proof of Theorem
3.1
is presented in Appendix
D
. It requires the use of further general notions about partitions on graphs, namely correlation length, connectedness, and strong connectedness. We leave the presentation of these notions to Appendix
C
, as they are not essential to Theorem
3.1
’s statement, but we do encourage the interested reader to get acquainted with them.

It can be seen in (
29
) that causal decompositions could be written in a more concise way by combining one step’s coarse-graining map with the next step’s fine-graining map. This yields maps between partitions on
\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}
that preserve the algebras respective to either the
\Gamma_{N}
or the
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
structure.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{in}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}}\vdash{{{\omega}}}^{\textrm{in}}
and
(\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{out}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}}\vdash{{{\omega}}}^{\textrm{out}}
be connected partitions. An isomorphism of C* algebras
\mathcal{U}:{{{\omega}}}^{\textrm{in}}\to{{{\omega}}}^{\textrm{out}}
is
\Gamma_{N}
-local
(resp.
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
-local) from the former to the latter partitions if for every
S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}
(resp.
\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
),

\mathcal{U}\left(\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{in}}_{S\pm\frac{1}{4}}\right)=\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{out}}_{S\pm\frac{1}{4}}\,.

(30)

A
\Gamma_{N}
-local isomorphism, for instance, can be represented as

\shortm\frac{1}{4}
\frac{1}{4}
\shortm\frac{3}{4}
\shortm\frac{5}{4}
\shortm\frac{7}{4}
\shortm\frac{9}{4}
\frac{5}{4}
\frac{3}{4}
\frac{9}{4}
\frac{7}{4}
\shortm\frac{1}{4}
\frac{1}{4}
\shortm\frac{3}{4}
\shortm\frac{5}{4}
\shortm\frac{7}{4}
\shortm\frac{9}{4}
\frac{5}{4}
\frac{3}{4}
\frac{9}{4}
\frac{7}{4}

(31)

while a

-local one has the same form, shifted to the right by
\frac{1}{2}
.

Corollary 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a 1D QCA between factorisations over
\Gamma_{N}
, with causality radius
r
,with
N>4r
.

There exist a fine-graining
\mathcal{U}^{\textrm{fine}}
, a coarse-graining
\mathcal{U}^{\textrm{coarse}}
, and
2r-1
isomorphisms of C*-algebras
\mathcal{V}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{2r-1}
, alternately
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
-local and
\Gamma_{N}
-local, such that

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}^{\textrm{coarse}}\mathcal{V}_{2r-1}\ldots\mathcal{V}_{1}\mathcal{U}^{\textrm{fine}}\,.

(32)

This decomposition is simply obtained by taking
\mathcal{V}_{i}:=\mathcal{U}_{i+1}^{\textrm{fine}}\,\,\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\textrm{coarse}}
in Theorem
3.1
. It can be represented as

\shortm 2
\shortm 1
0
1
2
\shortm 2
\shortm 1
0
1
2

(33)

3.2
The translation-invariant case

QCAs are often assumed to satisfy, on top of the causality condition, a condition of translation invariance. As we saw, this additional assumption is not necessary to derive our result. However, in the translation-invariant case, we will now show that the elements in the causal decomposition can themselves be taken to be translation invariant.

Let us start by formally introducing translation-invariance in the context of our presentation of QCAs. Because we view subsystems in a top-down way using partitions, there is no
a priori
, natural way to see translations on these; one has to specify by hand a partition’s preferred translation structure, given by a shift automorphism. For
S
a subset of
[0,N[
, we define the shifted set
S^{\textrm{Sh}}:=\{n+1\textrm{ mod }N\,|\,n\in S\}
.

Definition 3.3

(Shift automorphism)
.

Let
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma}\vdash{{{\omega}}}
be a connected partition, with
\Gamma\in\{\Gamma_{N},\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}\}
. A
shift automorphism
for it is a C* automorphism
Sh
of
{{{\omega}}}
satisfying

\forall S\subseteq\Gamma,\quad\textrm{Sh}(\mathcal{A}_{S})=\mathcal{A}_{S^{\textrm{Sh}}}\,,

(34a)

\textrm{Sh}^{N}=\mathcal{I}\,,

(34b)

where
\mathcal{I}
is the identy automorphism.

Note that no shift automorphism can exist for a partition whose individual algebras are not all isomorphic.

Definition 3.4

(Translation-Invariance)
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a quantum cellular automaton, or a fine- or coarse-graining, or a
\Gamma_{N}
- or
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
- local isomorphism. We suppose its input and output partitions are equipped with preferred shift automorphisms, respectively
\textrm{Sh}^{\textrm{in}}
and
\textrm{Sh}^{\textrm{out}}
.
\mathcal{U}
is
translation-invariant
if

\mathcal{U}\,\textrm{Sh}^{\textrm{in}}=\textrm{Sh}^{\textrm{out}}\,\mathcal{U}\,.

(35)

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a translation-invariant 1D QCA between factorisations over
\Gamma_{N}
, with causality radius
r
, with
N>4r
. Then all of the isomorphisms in the decompositions of Theorem
3.1
and Corollary
3.1
can be chosen to be translation-invariant.

4
Causal decompositions in routed unitary circuits

4.1
Routed maps and index-matching circuits

Our causal decompositions were so far expressed as compositions of C* algebra isomorphisms, which can be an unwieldy level of abstraction. Moreover, these isomorphisms in the decomposition were required to satisfy somewhat opaque conditions, such as (
30
). To make the decomposition wieldier, we need to map it back to the level of unitary maps between Hilbert spaces. This can be achieved, in spite of the non-factorness involved, using the language of
index-matching circuits

[
6
,
7
]
. We provide a basic introduction; detailed discussions, focused on the link with non-factor C*-algebras, are available in Ref.
[
21
]
.

We call a space
\mathcal{H}_{A}=\bigoplus_{k\in K}\mathcal{H}_{A}^{k}
equipped with a preferred (finite) sectorisation a
sectorised Hilbert space
.
5
5

5

A subtle difference with Ref.
[
6
]
is that we allow for some of these sectors to be null; as we explain in Appendix
F.3
, this is a structurally innocuous modification that allows for a simplification of the representations of the causal decompositions.
Sectorised Hilbert spaces can be tensored together in the standard way. A
relation

\lambda:K\to L
, where
K
and
L
are two sets, is a function from
K
to the powerset of
L
; it should be thought of as a generalisation of a function to being possibly multi- or empty-valued on each input, and can equivalently be represented as a Boolean matrix
(\lambda_{k}^{l})_{k\in K}^{l\in L}
, where each coefficient
\lambda_{k}^{l}
is equal to
1
if
\lambda
relates
k
to
l
, and
0
otherwise (we denote input indices as subscripts and output ones as superscripts).

A routed map
(\lambda,f)
from
\bigoplus_{k\in K}\mathcal{H}_{A}^{k}
to
\bigoplus_{l\in L}\mathcal{H}_{B}^{l}
consists of a relation
\lambda:K\to L
and a linear map
f:\mathcal{H}_{A}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}
such that
f

follows the route

\lambda
, i.e. such that for every
k
and
l
,
\lambda_{k}^{l}=0
implies that the
\mathcal{H}_{A}^{k}\to\mathcal{H}_{B}^{l}
block of
f
is null. This routed map is represented diagrammatically as

For a more involved example, taking
\mathcal{H}_{X}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in K\\
m\in M\end{subarray}}\mathcal{H}_{X}^{km}
,
\mathcal{H}_{Y}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in K\\
l\in L\end{subarray}}\mathcal{H}_{Y}^{kl}
and
\mathcal{H}_{Z}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}l\in L\\
m\in M\end{subarray}}\mathcal{H}_{Z}^{lm}
, and the route
\Delta:K\times M\to K\times L\times L\times M
defined by
\Delta_{km}^{k^{\prime}ll^{\prime}m^{\prime}}=\delta_{k}^{k^{\prime}}\delta^{ll^{\prime}}\delta_{m}^{m^{\prime}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\quad;

(38)

note how we write two indices next to a wire to indicate that the corresponding Hilbert space’s sectorisation is indexed by their Cartesian product.

The same notation is used for circuit diagrams representing sequential and parallel compositions of several routed maps; these diagrams are called
index-matching circuits
. Standard quantum circuits are accommodated into index-matching circuits, with un-indexed wires denoting Hilbert spaces with a trivial sectorisation.

A final diagrammatic simplification is that in an index-matching circuit, the repetition of an index over global input (resp. output) wires that are not otherwise connected through the graph implicitly means that the process is pre- (resp. post-) composed with the orthogonal projector enforcing equality of the values of that index. For instance, the index-matching circuit

k
m
l
l
k
m
l
l
f
g

(39)

is to be interpreted as the depicted tensor product of maps, pre-composed with the projector onto
\bigoplus_{k,l,m}\mathcal{H}_{Y}^{kl}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Z}^{lm}\subsetneq\mathcal{H}_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Z}
.

In a routed map
(\lambda,f)
,
\lambda
’s empty-valued inputs (resp. never-reached outputs) indicate sectors on which (resp. to which)
f
is null. For instance, in the example of (
38
), the form of
\lambda
indicates that
f
never reaches the sectors of the form
\mathcal{H}_{Y}^{kl}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Z}^{l^{\prime}m}
for
l\neq l^{\prime}
. We say that
(\lambda,f)
is a
routed unitary
if
f
is unitary once we have excluded such sectors from its input and output spaces; for instance (
38
) is a routed unitary if it is unitary from
\mathcal{H}_{X}
to
\bigoplus_{k,l,m}\mathcal{H}_{Y}^{kl}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Z}^{lm}\subsetneq\mathcal{H}_{Y}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{Z}
.

While routed maps can be composed in sequence and in parallel via pairwise composition, and thus form a circuit theory
[
6
]
, this is not necessarily the case for routed unitaries. In the case of index-matching, however, there is a direct characterisation of the well-behaved circuits in which routed unitarity is preserved. In the graph of an index-matching circuit diagram, we say that a vertex is a
starting point
for a certain repeated index if that index appears on at least one of its output wires but on none of its input wires; and that it is an
endpoint
for the index in the symmetric case. We say that the diagram is consistent if every index in it has exactly one starting point and one endpoint.
6
6

6

Note that this enforces that the diagram’s global input and output wires feature no indices, which is sufficient for our needs here (hence why we simply say it yields a unitary map). However the notion can be extended to the case in which these bear indices
[
6
]
.

Proposition 4.1
.

A consistent index-matching circuit in which all of the individual maps are routed unitaries represents a unitary map.

This is formally proven in Ref.
[
6
]
’s Theorem 20. For instance, the following index-matching diagram is consistent.

k
l
l
k
k
k
l
l

(40)

Finally, consistent index-matching unitary circuits are sound for causal reasoning, in the following sense.

Proposition 4.2
.

If, in a unitary
U
decomposed as a consistent index-matching unitary circuit, there is no forward path between an input and an output, then
U
features no causal influence from this input to this output.

This is proven in Ref.
[
5
]
’s Appendix A.10.

4.2
Representations of 1D partitions

(Although important to understand where the causal decomposition of the next subsection comes from, the results of this subsection are not directly needed for their statement; so the hurried reader can skip it.)

Using routed maps, we can craft specific non-factor partitions by taking algebras of operators over a tensor product of sectorised Hilbert spaces. For the cases of interest to this paper, we do it in the following way. Fixing an
N
, we define for every
n\in\Gamma_{N}
a sectorised Hilbert space whose sectors are labelled with two indices: a ‘left’ index
k_{n}^{\textrm{L}}\in K_{n}^{\textrm{L}}
and a ‘right’ index
k_{n}^{\textrm{R}}\in K_{n}^{\textrm{R}}
:

\mathcal{H}_{A_{n}}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{n}^{\textrm{L}}\in K_{n}^{\textrm{L}}\\
k_{n}^{\textrm{R}}\in K_{n}^{\textrm{R}}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{H}_{A_{n}}^{k_{n}^{\textrm{L}}k_{n}^{\textrm{R}}}\,,

(41)

with the requirement that
K_{n}^{\textrm{L}}=K_{n-1}^{\textrm{R}}
for every
n
; in other words, each site’s right-index takes value in the same set as the left-index of its neighbour to the right. In fact, we will in practice only use the subspace of
\bigotimes_{n}\mathcal{H}_{A_{n}}
in which these values are equal:
7
7

7

We denote the common value of
k_{n}^{\textrm{R}}
and
k_{n+1}^{\textrm{L}}
as
k_{n+\frac{1}{2}}
.

\begin{split}\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}&:=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{\frac{1}{2}}\in K_{0}^{\textrm{R}}\\
k_{\frac{3}{2}}\in K_{1}^{\textrm{R}}\\
\ldots\end{subarray}}\mathcal{H}_{A_{0}}^{k_{-\frac{1}{2}}k_{\frac{1}{2}}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A_{1}}^{k_{\frac{1}{2}}k_{\frac{3}{2}}}\otimes\ldots\\
&\subsetneq\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}^{\textrm{ext}}:=\bigotimes_{n\in\Gamma_{N}}\mathcal{H}_{A_{n}}\,.\end{split}

(42)

Using routed maps, we can then define the (factor) algebra
{{{\omega}}}
of operators over
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
as living within
\Lin(\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}^{\textrm{ext}})
: it is the set of operators of the form

(43)

where the route depicted by the index repetitions precisely enforces that
f
, though formally acting on
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}^{\textrm{ext}}
, has null components on and to the orthogonal complement of
\mathcal{H}_{\Omega}
.

We can then define sub-C* algebras of
{{{\omega}}}
, each corresponding to a collection of sites
S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}
, in the following way. Given an interval
\llbracket m,n\rrbracket\subseteq\Gamma_{N}
, we define
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}
as the set of maps on
\mathcal{H}_{A_{m}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{H}_{A_{n}}
that

1.

have to preserve the value of
A_{m}
’s left-index and
A_{n}
’s right-index;

2.

for any
l\in\llbracket m,n-1\rrbracket
, can change the common value of
A_{l}
’s right-hand index and
A_{l+1}
’s left-hand index, as long as this value remains the same for both indices.

Index-matching circuits make this graphically natural:
\mathcal{A}_{0}
,
\mathcal{A}_{\{0,1\}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,2\rrbracket}
, for instance, respectively take the form
