&=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\,.\end{split}

(88b)

More specifically, taking commutants suffices to find that (
86a
) and (
88a
) are equivalent; and, under the assumption of the former, (
86b
) (or its simplified form (
87
)) is equivalent to (
88b
). Let us prove this latter equivalence; given the obvious symmetry, showing implication one way is sufficient. We assume (
87
) holds. First,

\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&\quad\quad\overset{(\ref{eq: inc right})}{=}\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 1,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&\quad\quad\overset{(\ref{eq: comm of a part})}{=}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},0\rrbracket}\right)\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&\quad\quad=\Big((\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\shortm}})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{{p_{\shortm}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}})\\
&\quad\quad\quad\,\,\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},0\rrbracket}\,\,\Big)\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&\quad\quad=\left((\mathcal{Z}_{{p_{\shortm}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}})\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},0\rrbracket}\right)\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&\quad\quad\subseteq\left((\mathcal{Z}_{{p_{\shortm}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}})\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},0\rrbracket}\right)\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&\quad\quad=(\mathcal{Z}_{{p_{\shortm}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}})\vee\left(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},0\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\overset{(\ref{eq: restric qpl})}{=}(\mathcal{Z}_{{p_{\shortm}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}})\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\,.\end{split}

(89)

As the reverse inclusion is clear, this is in fact an equality. In the penultimate line, we used Lemma
D.8
with
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
, taking advantage of the fact that
d(\{{p_{\shortm}},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\},\{{q_{\shortm}},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\})\geq l
.

This shows that the commutant of
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
within
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
is
(\mathcal{Z}_{{p_{\shortm}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}})\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}=(\mathcal{Z}_{{p_{\shortm}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1})\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}=\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
, where the first equality comes from the fact that
(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1})\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-1\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
. Yet,
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
’s commutant within
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
is equal to
\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
’s commutant within the same, since the added first term commutes with all of
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
by definition. Thus,
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
’s commutant within
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
is equal to
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
’s double commutant within the same, which by Proposition
B.3
is equal to
\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}=(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\shortm}})\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
. We can use this to compute that (
88b
) holds:

\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&\quad\,\,=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&\quad\,\,=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\left((\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\shortm}})\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\right)\\
&\quad\,\,=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\\
&\quad\,\,\overset{(\ref{eq: inc right})}{=}\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\,,\end{split}

(90)

where, in the penultimate equality,
d(\{{q_{\shortm}}-1,{q_{\shortm}}\},\{0,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\})\geq l
allowed us to use Lemma
D.7
with
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
.

Finally, let us display a few formulas that stem from our assumptions and definitions, and will be useful to refer to. We fix
m\in\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket
and
n\in\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket
.

\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-1\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,0\rrbracket}\,;

(91a)

\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\,;

(91b)

\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,0\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\,;

(91c)

\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\,.

(91d)

(
91a
) is simply the combination of
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
’s definition with (
86a
), while (
91c
) is its combination with (
87
). The two other equations are symmetric.

More generally, we can infer from this the general rules for any
S,T\subseteq\Gamma^{\textrm{split}}
:

S\subseteq T\quad\implies\quad\mathcal{A}_{S}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{T}\,;

(92a)

S\cap T=\emptyset\quad\implies\quad\mathcal{A}_{T}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{S}^{\prime}\,.

(92b)

Indeed, if both
S
and
T
contain either none or both of the
\pm\frac{1}{4}
, then these two properties come from the fact that the original partition is indeed a partition (using Proposition
B.4
for the second). Otherwise, supposing they are intervals, enumerating the few possible cases and applying (
91
) proves the result. Finally when they are not, one can also infer that the formulas hold from the fact that they are algebraic spans of their connected components’ algebras.

We now turn to Lemma
D.9
’s proof.

Proof.

We have to prove that for any disjoint
S,T\subseteq\Gamma^{\textrm{split}}
,
(\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{split}}_{S},\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{split}}_{T})\vdash\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{split}}_{S\sqcup T}
. If neither
S
nor
T
contains any of the
\pm\frac{1}{4}
, or if one of them contains both, then given bullet point 1 above, this is direct from the fact that
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}}
is a partition. Otherwise, the crucial cases are the ones where
S
and
T
are intervals, so we will start with them.

A first case to prove is:
\forall m\in\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket,\forall n\in\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket,(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket},\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}
. We will use Proposition
B.7
with
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}=\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{2}=\mathcal{Z}_{n}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{n+1}
; indeed, using (
92a
) and (
92b
),
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}
is then included in
\mathcal{A}_{m}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
, and is also included in
\mathcal{A}_{m-1}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,0\rrbracket}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}
, so
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
, and symmetrically
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}
, and
\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}=\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}\vee\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{2}
by strong connectedness. Proposition
B.7
shows that it is sufficient for us to prove:
\forall\pi\in\textrm{AtomProj}(\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m}),\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}=\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}
.

Before we start proving this equality, let us remark that the LHS can be rewritten as
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}
. Indeed, by hypothesis
\pi
is an orthogonal projector and is an element of
\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m}
which is included both in
\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
and in
\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}
. Applying Lemma
D.1
for both algebras lets us rewrite
\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\textrm{Im}(\hat{\pi})\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}
. The same move will occur again later for different algebras (for example, in (
93
), to show that
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}=\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}
).

We thus fix
\pi\in\textrm{AtomProj}(\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m})
. Let us first prove the reverse inclusion, which is the easier one:
\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}
. On the one hand, by (
92a
),
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}
so
\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}\subseteq\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}
. On the other hand, by their definition, we have
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}
, and additionally
\pi\in\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket m,0\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,0\rrbracket}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}
, so
\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}
. Combining the two inclusions yields
\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}
.

We now turn to the direct inclusion, and compute

\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}\\
&\overset{(\ref{eq: inc implies inc})}{=}\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}\\
&\overset{(\ref{eq: comm condition})}{=}\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\\
&\overset{(\ref{eq: inc implies inc})}{=}\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\\
&\overset{(*)}{=}\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\\
&=\pi\left(\mu\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\mu\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\right)\\
&\overset{(\ref{eq: restric qmin})}{=}\pi\left(\mu(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime})\cap\mu\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\right)\\
&\subseteq\pi\left(\mu\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\mu\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\right)\\
&\overset{(\ref{eq: inc implies inc})}{=}\pi\left(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\mu\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\right)\,;\\
\end{split}

(93)

we leave the justification of the starred equality for later. The fifth equality comes from the application of Lemma
D.3
with
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
, yielding
\mu\in\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket 0,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}=(\mathcal{Z}_{0}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{1})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{p_{\shortm}})
, and the penultimate line comes from Lemma
D.4
.

We now distinguish two cases. Suppose
m\in\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket
; then
d(m-1,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}})\geq l
, so by Lemma
D.6
,
\mu\in\mathcal{Z}_{-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}
; therefore, by Lemma
D.1
, the last line of (
93
) becomes

\begin{split}&\pi\left(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\cap\textrm{Im}(\hat{\mu})\right)\\
&\subseteq\pi\left(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\right)\\
&\overset{(\ref{eq: restric qmin all})}{=}\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}\,.\end{split}

(94)

The other case is
m\in\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},{q_{\shortm}}-1\rrbracket
; then
d(m,0)\geq l
, so by Lemma
D.6
,
\mu\in(\mathcal{Z}_{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{p_{\shortm}})
. Then by applying Lemma
D.5
with
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},n\rrbracket}
, we get a
\nu\in\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},n\rrbracket}=(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\shortm}})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{n}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{n+1})
such that
\pi(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\mu\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket})=\pi\mu(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\nu\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket})=\pi(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\nu\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket})
; yet
d({p_{\shortm}},{q_{\shortm}})\geq l
, so
\nu\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{n+1}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}
, and thus by Lemma
D.1
,
\nu\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\cap\textrm{Im}(\hat{\nu})
. Thus the last line of (
93
) becomes
\pi\left(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\cap\textrm{Im}(\hat{\nu})\right)
, which by the same reasoning as in (
94
) is included in
\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}
.

In both cases, we thus find
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}\subseteq\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}
, the direct inclusion we set out to prove.

Let us return to the justification of the starred equality in (
93
). Note that for the case
m={p_{\shortm}}
, (
93
) is correct as one can move from the second to the fourth equality: since
\pi\in\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}
, we have by Lemma
D.1

\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\textrm{Im}(\hat{\pi})=\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}=\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
. Therefore, the above reasoning shows that
(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket},\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},n\rrbracket}
. As our assumptions are completely symmetric with respect to a swap of the left and right directions, a symmetric reasoning would show that, for any
m\in\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket
,
(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket},\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
. Therefore, for
\pi\in\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m}
, applying Proposition
B.4
then gives
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}=\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket m,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}=(\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m})\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
. This shows that, for
\pi\in\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m}
,

\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\\
&\quad\quad=\left((\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m})\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\\
&\quad\quad=\left((\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m})\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\cap\textrm{Im}(\hat{\pi})\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\\
&\quad\quad=\pi\left((\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m})\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\quad\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\\
&\quad\quad=\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\,,\end{split}

(95)

where we used Lemma
D.1
in the third equality, and Lemma
B.3
in the fourth one. This is the proof of the starred equality in (
93
), and concludes the proof that
(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket},\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}
.

As an interlude, let us use this to compute
\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
for a given
m\in\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket
. We start with the case
m={q_{\shortm}}
. First, using (
92a
) then Proposition
B.4
, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}&\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},0\rrbracket}\\
&=\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},0\rrbracket}\vee\mathcal{A}_{0}\\
&=(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\shortm}})\vee\mathcal{A}_{0}\,.\end{split}

(96)

Furthermore,
\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime}
so

\begin{split}\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}&\subseteq((\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\shortm}})\vee\mathcal{A}_{0})\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&=(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\shortm}})\vee(\mathcal{A}_{0}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime})\\
&\overset{(\ref{eq: restric qpl all})}{=}(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\shortm}})\vee\mathcal{A}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\,,\end{split}

(97)

where we used Lemma
D.8
with
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
, since
d(\{{q_{\shortm}}-1,{q_{\shortm}}\},\{0,{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\})\geq l
. By symmetry, we also have
\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}+1})
.

Using Proposition
B.5
applied to
(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket},\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
, we find

\begin{split}&\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\vee\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\\
&\quad\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}+1})\vee\mathcal{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}\\
&\quad=(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\shortm}})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}+1})\vee\mathcal{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}\,.\end{split}

(98)

Combining (
97
) and (
98
), we find

\begin{split}&\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\\
&\quad\subseteq\left((\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\shortm}})\vee\mathcal{A}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\cap\Big((\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}+1})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\shortm}})\\
&\quad\quad\quad\vee\mathcal{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}\Big)\\
&\quad=\left((\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\shortm}})\vee\mathcal{A}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\right)\\
&\quad\quad\cap\left((\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\shortm}})\vee\mathcal{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}\right)\\
&\quad=(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\shortm}})\vee(\mathcal{A}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\frac{1}{4}})\\
&\quad=(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\shortm}})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{\frac{1}{4}})\,,\end{split}

(99)

where we first used Lemma
D.7
with
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},0\rrbracket}
, since
d(\{{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}+1\},\{{q_{\shortm}},0\})\geq l
; then Lemma
D.7
with
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{A}_{0}
, since
d(\{{q_{\shortm}}-1,{q_{\shortm}}\},0)\geq l
. The last equality comes from the fact that
\mathcal{A}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}=\mathcal{Z}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{\frac{1}{4}}
, since the two sets being intersected commute by (
92b
).

Since the reverse inclusion is direct, we get
\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}=(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\shortm}}-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{q_{\shortm}})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{\frac{1}{4}})
, and symmetrically,
\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}=(\mathcal{Z}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{\frac{1}{4}})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}+1})
.

Let us now turn to the general case, taking
m\in\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket
. By Proposition
B.5
and the fact that
(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket},\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
, we have
\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\vee\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket m,{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}=(\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{\frac{1}{4}})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}+1})
. Since
\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,0\rrbracket}
, we find furthermore

\begin{split}&\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\\
&\subseteq\Big((\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{\frac{1}{4}})\\
&\quad\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}+1})\Big)\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,0\rrbracket}\\
&=((\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{\frac{1}{4}}))\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,0\rrbracket}\\
&\overset{(\ref{eq: inc implies inc})}{=}(\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{\frac{1}{4}})\,,\end{split}

(100)

where in the middle equality we used Lemma
D.7
with
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,0\rrbracket}
, since
d(\{{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}+1\},\{m,0\})\geq l
. With the reverse inclusion being direct, we can conclude that

\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}=(\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{\frac{1}{4}})\,,

(101a)

\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}=(\mathcal{Z}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{\frac{1}{4}})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{n}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{n+1})\,,

(101b)

where we added the symmetric case for
n\in\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket
.

This proves that the refined partition is a strongly connected one. It will also be useful for the next step in our proof, to which we now turn: showing that for any given
m\in\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-1\rrbracket
and
n\in\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket
,
(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n-1\rrbracket},\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
. Here again we will make use of Proposition
B.7
, with
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}=\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{2}=\mathcal{Z}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{\frac{1}{4}}
. (
101a
) indeed yields that
\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}=\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}\vee\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{2}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket n,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
, while
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket m,n-1\rrbracket}
comes from the strong connectedness of the initial partition.

The proposition shows that it is sufficient for us to prove
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}=\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
for
\pi\in\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m}
. Since the reverse inclusion is easy to prove, we focus on the direct one, and compute

\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\\
&\qquad\overset{(\ref{eq: inc implies inc})}{\subseteq}\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&\qquad\overset{(\ref{eq: inc implies inc})}{\subseteq}\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,0\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&\qquad\overset{(\ref{eq: eq def partition})}{=}\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n,0\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&\qquad=\pi(\mu\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n,0\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime})\\
&\qquad=\pi(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n,0\rrbracket}\cap\textrm{Im}(\hat{\mu})\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime})\\
&\qquad\subseteq\pi(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n,0\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime})\\
&\qquad=\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\,,\end{split}

(102)

where we made use of Lemma
D.5
with
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n,{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
, and then took advantage of the fact that, since
d(m-1,{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}})\geq l
, Lemma
D.6
yields
\mu\in\mathcal{Z}_{n-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n,0\rrbracket}
, so we can use Lemma
D.1
.

By symmetry, we also have, for all
m\in\llbracket 1,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket
and
n\in\llbracket\frac{1}{4},m\rrbracket
,
(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket},\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n+1,m\rrbracket})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},m\rrbracket}
.

We now turn to the cases involving ‘big’ intervals, those of size larger than
\frac{N}{2}
, which were defined as commutants of the small intervals. Let us start by proving that, for
m\in\llbracket 2,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket
,
n\in\llbracket\frac{1}{4},m-2\rrbracket
,
(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket},\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}
. Here again, using Proposition
B.7
, it is sufficient to prove that for any
\pi\in\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m}
,
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}=\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}
. Since the reverse inclusion is easy to prove, we focus on the direct one. It is given by the computation

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}&\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}\\
&=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},m-1\rrbracket}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}\\
&=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,m-1\rrbracket}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}\\
&=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}\\
&=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\\
&\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\\
&=\pi\left(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\mu\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\right)\\
&=\pi\left(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\cap\textrm{Im}(\hat{\mu})\right)\\
&\subseteq\pi\left(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}\right)\\
&=\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}\,,\end{split}

(103)

where we used Lemma
D.5
with
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},n\rrbracket}
, then Lemma
D.1
, since Lemma
D.6
proves that
\mu\in\mathcal{Z}_{n}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{n+1}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket}
as
d(m-1,{q_{\shortm}})\geq l
.

Finally, we have to prove that, for
m\in\llbracket 1,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket
and
n\in\llbracket m+1,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket
,
(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n-1\rrbracket},\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
. Like before, we only have to prove that for
\pi\in\mathcal{Z}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{m}
,
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\subseteq\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
. This is given simply by

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}&\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\\
&=\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\\
&=\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n,m-1\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},m-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&=\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\,,\end{split}

(104)

where we used Lemma
D.1
, and where the last line comes from (
102
) or (
103
), depending on whether
n\in\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket
or
n\in\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket
. The symmetric case gives an analogous result.

This concludes the proof that
(\mathcal{A}_{S},\mathcal{A}_{T})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{S\sqcup T}
when
S
,
T
, and
S\sqcup T
are all intervals. Let us now turn to the case in which only
S\sqcup T
is an interval. We write it as
\llbracket m_{0},n\rrbracket
;
19
19

19

The case in which
S\sqcup T
is the whole graph works in the same way: under an analogous induction hypothesis, we then directly have (
105
) by taking
\llbracket m_{0},m_{1}\rrbracket
to be an arbitrary connected component of
S
.
then, taking without loss of generality
m_{0}\in S
and writing
\llbracket m_{0},m_{1}\rrbracket
as the connected component of
S
containing
m_{0}
, we have
S=\llbracket m_{0},m_{1}\rrbracket\sqcup\tilde{S}
such that
\tilde{S}\sqcup T=\llbracket m_{1}+1,n\rrbracket
. Then, if we suppose that
(\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{S}},\mathcal{A}_{T})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m_{1}+1,n\rrbracket}
, we get, for
\pi\in\textrm{AtomProj}(\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket m_{0},n\rrbracket})
,

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{S}^{\prime}&\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m_{0},n\rrbracket}\\
&=(\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{S}}\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m_{0},m_{1}\rrbracket})^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m_{0},n\rrbracket}\\
&=\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{S}}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m_{0},m_{1}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m_{0},n\rrbracket}\\
&=\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{S}}^{\prime}\cap\pi\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m_{1}+1,n\rrbracket}\\
&=\pi\left(\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{S}}^{\prime}\cap\mu\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m_{1}+1,n\rrbracket}\right)\\
&=\pi\mu\mathcal{A}_{T}\\
&=\pi\mathcal{A}_{T}\,,\\
\end{split}

(105)

where we used the fact that
(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m_{0},m_{1}\rrbracket},\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m_{1}+1,n\rrbracket})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m_{0},n\rrbracket}
, then Lemma
D.5
with
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m_{1}+1,n\rrbracket}
, then our assumption that
(\mathcal{A}_{\tilde{S}},\mathcal{A}_{T})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m_{1}+1,n\rrbracket}
. This gives an induction on the size of
S\sqcup T
, allowing us to conclude that
(\mathcal{A}_{S},\mathcal{A}_{T})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{S\sqcup T}
always holds when
S\sqcup T
is an interval.

Finally, let us consider the general case. We can then decompose
S\sqcup T=\bigsqcup_{i}\left(S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}\right)
where the
S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}
’s are non-contiguous intervals, and therefore
\mathcal{A}_{S\sqcup T}=\bigvee_{i}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}}
. This also implies
\mathcal{Z}_{S\sqcup T}=\bigvee_{i}\mathcal{Z}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}}
, so, by Lemma
B.2
, taking an atomic projector
\pi
of
\mathcal{A}_{S\sqcup T}
, it is a product
\prod_{i}\pi_{i}
of atomic projectors of each of the
\mathcal{Z}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}}
’s. We then compute

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{S}^{\prime}&\cap\mathcal{A}_{S\sqcup T}=\left(\bigvee_{i}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}}\right)^{\prime}\cap\pi\left(\bigvee_{i}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}}\right)\\
&=\left(\bigcap_{i}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}}^{\prime}\right)\cap\left(\prod_{i}\pi_{i}\right)\left(\bigvee_{i}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}}\right)\\
&=\left(\bigcap_{i}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}}^{\prime}\right)\cap\left(\bigvee_{i}\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}}\right)\,;\end{split}

(106)

the third line is justified by the fact that an element of
(\prod_{i}\pi_{i})(\bigvee_{i}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}})
can be written as
(\prod_{i}\pi_{i})(\sum_{t}\prod_{i}f_{i}^{t})=\sum_{t}\prod_{i}(\pi_{i}f_{i}^{t})
, so
(\prod_{i}\pi_{i})(\bigvee_{i}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}})=\bigvee_{i}\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}}
.

Since the
\pi_{i}
’s are by assumption atomic projectors of each of the
\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}}
’s, each of the
\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}}
’s is a factor. Thus, the homomorphism of C*-algebras from
\bigotimes_{i}\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}}
to
{{{\omega}}}
given by the product of elements is on a factor domain; so by Proposition
B.1
, being non-null, it is injective.

Writing an element of (
107
) as
f=\sum_{t}f_{0}^{t}\tilde{f}^{t}
, where the
\tilde{f}^{t}
’s form a basis of
\bigvee_{i\neq 0}\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}}
and the
f_{0}^{t}
’s are in
\mathcal{A}_{S_{0}\sqcup T_{0}}
, we have for any
g\in\mathcal{A}_{S_{0}}
:
0=[g,f]=\sum_{t}[g,f_{0}^{t}]\tilde{f}^{t}
, which by injectiveness of the product homomorphism yields
\forall t,[g,f_{0}^{t}]=0
, so (
107
)’s RHS is equal to
(\mathcal{A}_{S_{0}}^{\prime}\cap\pi_{0}\mathcal{A}_{S_{0}\sqcup T_{0}})\vee((\bigcap_{i\neq 0}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}}^{\prime})\cap(\bigvee_{i\neq 0}\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}}))
. Iterating the procedure for every
i
yields
\bigvee_{i}\left(\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}}^{\prime}\cap\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}}\right)
, so we can finish the computation:

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{S}^{\prime}&\cap\mathcal{A}_{S\sqcup T}=\bigvee_{i}\left(\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}}^{\prime}\cap\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}}\right)\\
&=\bigvee_{i}\pi_{i}\mathcal{A}_{T_{i}}\\
&=\left(\prod_{i}\pi_{i}\right)\left(\bigvee_{i}\mathcal{A}_{T_{i}}\right)\\
&=\pi\mathcal{A}_{\bigsqcup_{i}T_{i}}\\
&=\pi\mathcal{A}_{T}\,,\end{split}

(107)

where we used the fact that
(\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}},\mathcal{A}_{T_{i}})\vdash\mathcal{A}_{S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}}
since
S_{i}\sqcup T_{i}
is an interval, then the fact that
\mathcal{A}_{\bigsqcup_{i}T_{i}}=\bigvee_{i}\mathcal{A}_{T_{i}}
since the
T_{i}
’s are non-contiguous. This concludes the proof.
∎

D.3
Fine-graining

We can leverage Lemma
D.9
to obtain the fine-graining theorem that will serve as the induction step for a proof of Theorem
3.1
.
20
20

20

In the theorem’s statement, we added the possibility for the
\mathcal{B}
-partition not to be a factorisation for the sake of generality; but for the proof of our main result, one only needs the case in which it is a factorisation, i.e. the case
l_{B}=0
.

Theorem D.1

(Fine-graining)
.

Let
N
be a natural number, and
r
either a natural number or half a natural number. In the first case, we take
\Gamma=\Gamma_{N}
, and in the other,
\Gamma=\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
. Suppose
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}}
and
(\mathcal{B}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma}
are two strongly connected 1D partitions of a factor
{{{\omega}}}
, of correlation length at most
l_{A}
and
l_{B}
respectively, satisfying

\forall S\subseteq\Gamma_{N},\quad\mathcal{A}_{S}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{S\pm r}\,.

(108)

If
N\geq 4r+2(l_{A}+l_{B})+1
, then there exists a strongly connected 1D partition
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}}\vdash{{{\omega}}}
, with correlation length at most
l_{A}+\frac{1}{2}
, satisfying

\forall S\subseteq\Gamma_{N},\quad\mathcal{A}_{S}=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S\pm\frac{1}{4}}\,;

(109a)

\forall S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}},\quad\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{S\pm(r-\frac{3}{4})}\,.

(109b)

Proof.

The idea is to build
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}}
from
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}}
by repeatedly using Lemma
D.9
to split each of
\Gamma
’s vertices in two, one by one.
21
21

21

Note that, strictly speaking, Lemma
D.9
was written as starting with a partition on
\Gamma_{N}
(or on the isomorphic
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
). However, its result also holds when applied to a partition in which some vertices have been split already, i.e. a partition on a graph intermediary between
\Gamma_{N}
and
\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}
, in which some of the
n\in\Gamma_{N}
have been replaced with two vertices
n\pm\frac{1}{4}
, and some have not.
We deliberately refrained from writing it in this way, as defining and manipulating such partly-split graphs would have brought to the proof much formal burden at the detriment of the clarity of the main arguments.
As a first step, for instance, the split of the vertex
0
is obtained by taking

\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}:=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},0\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket r,r+l_{B}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\,,

(110)

and more generally, for the step in which the vertex
n
is split, we similarly take

\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n+{p_{\shortm}},n-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}:=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n+{p_{\shortm}},n\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket n+r,n+r+l_{B}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\,.

(111)

Let us focus on the first step, as every step is completely analogous. We first prove that the assumptions of Lemma
D.9
are then satisfied. We start with the assumption that
N\geq 4l_{A}+1
. For any
S\subseteq\Gamma
, we have
\mathcal{A}_{S}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{S\pm r}\,\,\bot\,\,\mathcal{B}_{\overline{S\pm(r+l_{B})}}\supseteq\mathcal{B}_{\overline{S\pm(2r+l_{B})}\pm r}\supseteq\mathcal{A}_{\overline{S\pm(2r+l_{B})}}
; thus
l_{A}\leq l_{B}+2r
. Together with our assumption that
N\geq 4r+2(l_{A}+l_{B})+1
, this yields
N\geq 4l_{A}+1
.

Turning to (
86a
), we have by definition
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},0\rrbracket}
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-1\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}-(r-1),r-1\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket r+l_{B}+1,r-1\rrbracket}=\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket r,l_{B}\rrbracket}^{\prime}
(the second inclusion comes from the fact that
{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}=\left\lfloor\frac{N-1}{2}\right\rfloor\geq 2r+(l_{A}+l_{B})+1
and thus
{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}-(r-1)\geq r+l_{B}+1
). Therefore
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-1\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
.

As for (
86b
), it is proven by the fact that
\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket r,r+l_{B}\rrbracket}=\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket r+l_{B}+1,r-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 2r+l_{B}+1,-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,2r+l_{B}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
(the last inclusion comes once again from
{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\geq 2r+(l_{A}+l_{B})+1
, from which we derive
{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}:={p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}-l_{A}\geq 2r+l_{B}
). As by definition we also have
\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket r,r+l_{B}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}=:\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
, we find that
\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket r,r+l_{B}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
and thus

\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},0\rrbracket}\cap(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,{q_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket})^{\prime}\\
&\qquad\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},0\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket r,r+l_{B}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&\qquad=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket},\end{split}

(112)

and the reverse inclusion is direct.

We can thus use Lemma
D.9
with the specification (
110
) to get a new partition
\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{split}}
over the graph in which vertex
0
has been replaced with two vertices
\pm\frac{1}{4}
. Like before, we will denote
\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{split}}_{S}
as
\mathcal{A}_{S}
, since the presence of
\pm\frac{1}{4}
in
S
is sufficient to disambiguate one from the other. First, (
91a
) and (
91b
) tell us that for any
m
,
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,0\rrbracket}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},m\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket}
; after splitting every vertex, this yields (
109a
). To eventually obtain (
109b
), we need to prove, for any
m
, the following inclusions:

\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket m-r,r-1\rrbracket}\,;

(113a)

\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},m\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket r-1,m+r\rrbracket}\,.

(113b)

We start with (
113a
), for an
m\in\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket
. Then from (
108
) and (
110
), we get
22
22

22

We denote
\mathcal{Z}^{B}_{S}
for the centre of
\mathcal{B}_{S}
, to clarify that we are talking about centres of the
\mathcal{B}
algebras and not the
\mathcal{A}
ones.

\begin{split}&\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket m-r,r\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket r,r+l_{B}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&\quad\quad=\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket m-r,r\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket m-r,r+l_{B}\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket r,r+l_{B}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&\quad\quad=\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket m-r,r\rrbracket}\\
&\quad\quad\cap\left((\mathcal{Z}^{B}_{r+l_{B}}\cap\mathcal{Z}^{B}_{r+l_{B}+1})\vee\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket m-r,r-1\rrbracket}\right)\\
&\quad\quad=\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket m-r,r\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket m-r,r-1\rrbracket}\\
&\quad\quad=\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket m-r,r-1\rrbracket}\,,\end{split}

(114)

where the penultimate equality is given by Lemma
D.7
with
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket m-r,r\rrbracket}
, using
d(\{r+l_{B},r+l_{B}+1\},\{m-r,r\})\geq l_{B}
. For the proof of (
113b
) for
m\in\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket
, note that
\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket-(r+l_{B}),-r\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},0\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket r,r+l_{B}\rrbracket}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
. Thus a computation completely symmetric to the previous one yields the result.

Finally, we have the case of large intervals. First, let us prove (
113a
) for
m\in\llbracket 1,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket
. The previous results show that
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}}-r,r-1\llbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket m-r,r-1\rrbracket}
, so dually,
\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket r,m-r-1\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
. Since we also have
\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket r,m-r-1\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,m-1\rrbracket}
, we conclude that
\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket r,m-r-1\rrbracket}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},m-1\rrbracket}
, which is the dual of (
113a
). The proof of (
113b
) for
m\in\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-1\rrbracket
is symmetric.

After splitting every node in the same way, we obtain a partition
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}}
which can be specified in the following way: for
m,n\in\Gamma_{N}
satisfying
m\in\llbracket n+{p_{\shortm}},n-1\rrbracket
,

\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket m-\frac{1}{4},n+\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}:=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}\,;

(115a)

\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket m-\frac{1}{4},n-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}:=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket m+r,m+r+l_{B}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\,;

(115b)

\begin{split}&\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket m+\frac{1}{4},n+\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}:=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}\\
&\cap\left(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m+{p_{\shortm}},m\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket m+r,m+r+l_{B}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}\,;\end{split}

(115c)

\begin{split}&\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket m+\frac{1}{4},n-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\\
&:=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket n+r,n+r+l_{B}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&\cap\left(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m+{p_{\shortm}},m\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{B}_{\llbracket m+r,m+r+l_{B}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}\,,\end{split}

(115d)

while the specification for larger intervals is given by taking commutants, and that for non-intervals is given by (
67
).

Finally, let us check that the partition
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}}
obtained after splitting every node has correlation length at most
l_{A}+\frac{1}{2}
. This comes from the fact that for every
S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}
, we have
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{S\pm\frac{1}{4}}\,\,\bot\,\,\mathcal{A}_{\overline{S\pm(\frac{1}{4}+l_{A})}}\supseteq\mathcal{A}_{\overline{S\pm(\frac{1}{2}+l_{A})}\pm\frac{1}{4}}\supseteq\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\overline{S\pm(\frac{1}{2}+l_{A})}}
. Note that in the first inclusion there, we used (
109a
) in the other direction; we can do this because of the invertibility of QCAs with a given causality radius, proven in Proposition
2.1
.
∎

D.4
Envoi

Finally, we can prove Theorem
3.1
, in a slightly generalised form, in which the QCA does not necessarily have to be between factorisations. Besides the added generality, this makes the proof by induction smoother.

Theorem D.2
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a (generalised) 1D QCA with causality radius
r
, from a strongly connected partition of a factor, with correlation length at most
l
, to one with correlation length at most
l^{\prime}
, over
\Gamma_{N}
or
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
.

If
N>4r+2(l+l^{\prime})
, then there exist
2r
(generalised) 1D QCAs
\mathcal{U}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{2r}
, with causality radius
\frac{1}{2}
, such that

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{2r}\ldots\mathcal{U}_{1}\,.

(116)

Furthermore, each of the
\mathcal{U}_{i}
’s decomposes as

\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\textrm{coarse}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\textrm{fine}}\,,

(117)

where
\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\textrm{fine}}
is a fine-graining and
\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\textrm{coarse}}
is a coarse-graining.

Proof.

The proof is by induction. Suppose that the theorem is true up to a certain
r-\frac{1}{2}
, and take
\mathcal{U}
to be of radius
r
(or, for the base case, suppose nothing and take
r=\frac{1}{2}
). We write
\mathcal{U}
’s input partition as
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}}\vdash{{{\omega}}}
, and its output partition as
(\mathcal{B}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma}
(with
\Gamma
being
\Gamma_{N}
or
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
depending on whether
r
is an integer or half an integer). Then, as
\mathcal{U}
is a C*-algebra isomorphism and thus preserves inclusions and commutants, (
19
) is equivalent to (
108
) with
\mathcal{B}_{S}
’s replaced with
\mathcal{U}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}_{S})
’s. We can thus apply Theorem
D.1
, which yields a strongly connected partition
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}}\vdash{{{\omega}}}
with correlation length at most
l+\frac{1}{2}
, satisfying (
109a
) and (
109b
).

Let us then define
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{coarse}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}\vdash{{{\omega}}}
to be the partition over
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
obtained by coarse-graining
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}}
through

\forall S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},\quad\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{coarse}}_{S}:=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S\pm\frac{1}{4}}\,.

(118)

From the properties of
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}}
, it is then clear that
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{coarse}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}
is a strongly connected partition, with correlation length at most
l+1
, and that (
109b
) yields

\forall S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},\,\,\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{coarse}}_{S}\subseteq\mathcal{U}^{-1}\left(\mathcal{B}_{S\pm(r-\frac{1}{2})}\right)\,.

(119)

This means that
\mathcal{U}
, when seen as mapping from the partition
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{coarse}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}
to the partition
(\mathcal{B}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma}
, has radius
r-\frac{1}{2}
. Furthermore,
N>4r+2(l+l^{\prime})=4(r-\frac{1}{2})+2((l+1)+l^{\prime})
.

If we are in the base case, we find
\forall S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{coarse}}_{S}=\mathcal{U}^{-1}(\mathcal{B}_{S})
; so, defining
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\textrm{fine}}:=\mathcal{I}
(the identity automorphism on
{{{\omega}}}^{\textrm{in}}
) and
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\textrm{coarse}}:=\mathcal{U}
, and seeing the first one as mapping from
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}}
to
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}}
, and the second as mapping from the latter to
(\mathcal{B}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma}
, we find that the first is a fine-graining and the second a coarse-graining.

Otherwise, by our induction hypothesis, we can thus decompose
\mathcal{U}
(seen as mapping from
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{coarse}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}
to
(\mathcal{B}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma}
) as
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{2r}\ldots\mathcal{U}_{2}
. Furthermore, we can define
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\textrm{fine}}=\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\textrm{coarse}}=\mathcal{I}
; seeing the first one as going from the partition
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}}
to
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}}
, and the second as going from the latter to the partition
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{coarse}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}
, the first one is then a fine-graining and the second a coarse-graining,
23
23

23

It might feel awkward that the automorphisms in our decomposition are almost all identities, but this is in fact the crux of our proof strategy: focusing on the interplay between different partitions of the
same
algebra and on how, under the right conditions, it allows one to fine-grain these partitions. In this ‘passive’ picture, the ‘active’ components — the QCAs, yielding isomorphisms from one C*-algebra to another — play nothing more than a bookkeeping role.
and we then have

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}_{2r}\ldots\mathcal{U}_{2}\,\mathcal{U}_{1}\,.

(120)

∎

Theorem
3.1
is then simply the restriction of Theorem
D.2
to the case of QCAs between factorisations, for which we have
l=l^{\prime}=0
.

Appendix E
Proof of Theorem
3.2

Our proof by induction of Theorem
D.2
in Section
D.4
shows that each
\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\textrm{fine}}
or
\mathcal{U}_{i}^{\textrm{coarse}}
in this Theorem can be taken to be the identity automorphism on
{{{\omega}}}^{\textrm{in}}
(but typed as mapping between different partitions of it), except for
\mathcal{U}_{2r}^{\textrm{coarse}}
which is taken to be equal to
\mathcal{U}
. All we have to prove is thus that
\textrm{Sh}^{\textrm{in}}
is a shift automorphism (i.e. satisfies (
34a
)) with respect to each of the intermediate partitions.

To achieve this, let us prove that in Theorem
D.1
, any automorphism
Sh
of
{{{\omega}}}
that is a shift with respect to both the
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}}
and the
(\mathcal{B}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma}
partitions is also a shift with respect to the
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}}
partition. Indeed, the equations (
115
) (together with the specification via commutants and spans of unions for large intervals and for non-intervals) show that for any given
S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{A}_{S}}=F_{S}\left[(\mathcal{A}_{T})_{T\subseteq\Gamma_{N}},(\mathcal{B}_{T^{\prime}})_{T^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma}\right]
’s, where the
F_{S}
functions satisfy
F_{S}\left[(\mathcal{A}_{T^{\textrm{Sh}}})_{T\subseteq\Gamma_{N}},(\mathcal{B}_{(T^{\prime})^{\textrm{Sh}}})_{T^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma}\right]=F_{S^{\textrm{Sh}}}\left((\mathcal{A}_{T})_{T\subseteq\Gamma_{N}},(\mathcal{B}_{T^{\prime}})_{T^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma}\right)
, and, crucially, only involve commutants, intersections, and algebraic spans of union.

Therefore, as any C* automorphism distributes over these three operations, we find that if
Sh
is a shift for both the
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
partitions, then for an arbitrary
S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}
,

\begin{split}\textrm{Sh}\,\tilde{\mathcal{A}_{S}}&=\textrm{Sh}\left(F_{S}\left[(\mathcal{A}_{T})_{T\subseteq\Gamma_{N}},(\mathcal{B}_{T^{\prime}})_{T^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma}\right]\right)\\
&=F_{S}\left[(\textrm{Sh}\,\mathcal{A}_{T})_{T\subseteq\Gamma_{N}},(\textrm{Sh}\,\mathcal{B}_{T^{\prime}})_{T^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma}\right]\\
&=F_{S}\left[(\mathcal{A}_{T^{\textrm{Sh}}})_{T\subseteq\Gamma_{N}},(\mathcal{B}_{(T^{\prime})^{\textrm{Sh}}})_{T^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma}\right]\\
&=F_{S^{\textrm{Sh}}}\left[(\mathcal{A}_{T})_{T\subseteq\Gamma_{N}},(\mathcal{B}_{T^{\prime}})_{T^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma}\right]\\
&=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S^{\textrm{Sh}}}\,,\end{split}

(121)

so
Sh
is a shift with respect to
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}}
.

Returning to our proof by induction of Theorem
D.2
in Section
D.4
, we note that translation-invariance can be restated as the fact that
\textrm{Sh}^{\textrm{in}}
is a shift with respect to both
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}}
and
(\mathcal{U}^{{}^{-1}}(\mathcal{B}_{S}))_{S\subseteq\Gamma}
. Thus, by our previous result, it is also a shift with respect to
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{\textrm{fine}}}
, and thus also with respect to
(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{coarse}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}
, which is the former’s coarse-graining.

In the base case, we thus find that both
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\textrm{fine}}:=\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\textrm{coarse}}:=\mathcal{U}
are translation-invariant, where the input and output partitions of
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\textrm{fine}}
are equipped with the shift
\textrm{Sh}^{\textrm{in}}
; while in the case of the induction step,
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\textrm{fine}}:=\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\textrm{coarse}}:=\mathcal{I}
are translation-invariant, where all partitions involved are equipped with the shift
\textrm{Sh}^{\textrm{in}}
.

Appendix F
Proofs for Section
4

F.1
Some Lemmas

The following two lemmas tell us that in a strongly connected 1D partition over a sufficiently large graph, sectorial correlations — the fact that some sectors at different points are incompatible, witnessed by the fact that the corresponding projectors’ product is null — always come down to nearest-neighbour correlations: if the product of some projectors is null, it must be because two of them, at neighbouring edges, have a null product.

Note how, to better manipulate the structure of strong connectedness, we move from talking about centres at a vertex — the
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
’s for
n\in\Gamma_{N}
— to centres at an edge — the
\mathcal{Z}_{n-\frac{1}{2}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{n+\frac{1}{2}}
’s for
n\in\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
.

Lemma F.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}}
be a strongly connected 1D partition with correlation length at most
l
, where
N\geq 3l+1
. For every
n
in
\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
, we take
\pi_{n}\in\textrm{AtomProj}(\mathcal{Z}_{n-\frac{1}{2}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{n+\frac{1}{2}})
.

Then
\prod_{n\in\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}\pi_{n}=0
if and only if
\exists n\in\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}
such that
\pi_{n}\pi_{n+1}=0
.

Proof.

Let us suppose
\prod_{n\in\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}\pi_{n}=0
without any of the
\pi_{n}\pi_{n+1}
’s being null, and reach a contradiction. We denote
{p_{\shortm}}
and
{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}
as
0
’s antipodes, as in the statement of Lemma
D.9
. Then
d({p_{\shortm}},1)>l
, so
\pi_{\frac{1}{2}}\in\mathcal{Z}_{1}\,\,\bot\,\,\mathcal{Z}_{p_{\shortm}}\ni\pi_{{p_{\shortm}}-\frac{1}{2}}
, so
\pi_{{p_{\shortm}}-\frac{1}{2}}\pi_{\frac{1}{2}}\neq 0
.

Yet
\pi_{{p_{\shortm}}-\frac{1}{2}}\pi_{\frac{1}{2}}
is an atomic projector of
\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},0\rrbracket}=\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket 1,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
, so
\pi_{{p_{\shortm}}-\frac{1}{2}}\pi_{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},0\rrbracket}
and
\pi_{{p_{\shortm}}-\frac{1}{2}}\pi_{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 1,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
are commuting factors, so the composition of elements of one with elements of the other (defined as in (
62
)), not being uniformly null, is necessarily injective. Therefore
\prod_{n\in\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}\pi_{n}=0
implies that at least one of
\prod_{n\in\llbracket{p_{\shortm}}-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\rrbracket}\pi_{n}
and
\prod_{n\in\llbracket\frac{1}{2},{p_{\shortm}}-\frac{1}{2}\rrbracket}\pi_{n}
is null.

Suppose without loss of generality that
\prod_{n\in\llbracket\frac{1}{2},{p_{\shortm}}-\frac{1}{2}\rrbracket}\pi_{n}=0
. Since
0-{p_{\shortm}}\geq l
, Lemma
D.6
’s second item tells us that
\ker\hat{\pi}_{\frac{1}{2}}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 2,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}=\bar{\mu}\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 2,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}
with
\bar{\mu}=\mathbb{1}-\mu\in\mathcal{Z}_{1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{2}
. As
\pi_{\frac{3}{2}}
is an atomic projector of
\mathcal{Z}_{1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{2}
satisfying
\pi_{\frac{1}{2}}\pi_{\frac{3}{2}}\neq 0
,
\pi_{\frac{3}{2}}=\pi_{\frac{3}{2}}\mu
, so
\hat{\pi}_{\frac{1}{2}}
is injective on
\mu\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 2,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\ni\prod_{n\in\llbracket\frac{3}{2},{p_{\shortm}}-\frac{1}{2}\rrbracket}\pi_{n}
, so
\prod_{n\in\llbracket\frac{3}{2},{p_{\shortm}}-\frac{1}{2}\rrbracket}\pi_{n}=0
. This procedure can be repeated to erase terms from the product until we reach
\pi_{{p_{\shortm}}-\frac{1}{2}}=0
, which is a contradiction.
∎

Lemma F.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}}
be a strongly connected 1D partition with correlation length at most
l
, where
N\geq 3l+1
. We take
m,n\in\Gamma_{N}
, and for every
x\in\llbracket m-\frac{1}{2},n+\frac{1}{2}\rrbracket
, we take
\pi_{x}\in\textrm{AtomProj}(\mathcal{Z}_{x-\frac{1}{2}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{x+\frac{1}{2}})
.

Then
\prod_{x\in\llbracket m-\frac{1}{2},n+\frac{1}{2}\rrbracket}\pi_{x}=0
if and only if
\pi_{m-\frac{1}{2}}\pi_{n+\frac{1}{2}}=0
or
\exists x\in\llbracket m-\frac{1}{2},n-\frac{1}{2}\rrbracket,\pi_{x}\pi_{x+1}=0
.

Proof.

Suppose
\pi_{m-\frac{1}{2}}\pi_{n+\frac{1}{2}}\neq 0
; it is then an atomic projector of
\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket n+1,m-1\rrbracket}
, so
\pi_{m-\frac{1}{2}}\pi_{n+\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n+1,m-1\rrbracket}
is a non-zero factor algebra, and we have
(\pi_{m-\frac{1}{2}}\pi_{n+\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq\llbracket n+1,m-1\rrbracket}\vdash\pi_{m-\frac{1}{2}}\pi_{n+\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n+1,m-1\rrbracket}
. The identity is thus in
\bigvee_{t\in\llbracket n+1,m-1\rrbracket}\mathcal{Z}(\pi_{m-\frac{1}{2}}\pi_{n+\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{A}_{t})=\bigvee_{t\in\llbracket n+1,m-1\rrbracket}\pi_{m-\frac{1}{2}}\pi_{n+\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{Z}_{t}=\bigvee_{x\in\llbracket n+\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}\rrbracket}\pi_{m-\frac{1}{2}}\pi_{n+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{Z}_{x-\frac{1}{2}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{x+\frac{1}{2}})
(the first equality uses Lemma
B.1
); in particular the latter cannot be null, so we can pick
\pi_{x}\in\textrm{AtomProj}(\mathcal{Z}_{x-\frac{1}{2}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{x+\frac{1}{2}})
for every
x\in\llbracket n+\frac{3}{2},m-\frac{3}{2}\rrbracket
such that
\prod_{x\in\llbracket n+\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}\rrbracket}\pi_{x}\neq 0
. Composition with the latter is necessarily an injective homomorphism on the factor
\pi_{m-\frac{1}{2}}\pi_{n+\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket m,n\rrbracket}\ni\prod_{x\in\llbracket m-\frac{1}{2},n+\frac{1}{2}\rrbracket}\pi_{x}
; so
\prod_{x\in\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}}}\pi_{x}\neq 0
. We can then apply Lemma
F.1
to get the result.
∎

F.2
Full representability of the partitions

Here, we adopt the notations and results of Ref.
[
21
]
, to which the reader can refer for more details. As shown by Ref.
[
21
]
’s Theorem 5.2, any partition admits a concrete representation as a set of (routed) operators over a tensor product of Hilbert spaces, in which its individual parts’ algebras — i.e. the
\mathcal{A}_{x}
’s corresponding to singletons — are well-localised, i.e. of the form

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{x}=\Bigg\{\,\Big(f_{A_{x}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{A_{x}^{\prime},\,x^{\prime}\neq x}\Big)\,\,&\tilde{\mathbb{\Pi}}\\
\quad\Bigg|\,\,f\in\Lin_{\delta}\left(\mathcal{H}_{A_{x}}\right)&\Bigg\}\,;\end{split}

(122)

however, it might not be the same for the algebras corresponding to composite systems. We say that the partition is
fully representable
if the representation can furthermore be picked such that

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{S}=\Bigg\{\,\Big(f_{A_{n},\,n\in S}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{A_{n},\,n\not\in S}\Big)\,\,\,&\tilde{\mathbb{\Pi}}\\
\quad\Bigg|\,\,f\in\Lin_{\eta_{S}}\left(\bigotimes_{n\in S}\mathcal{H}_{A_{n}}\right)&\Bigg\}\,,\end{split}

(123)

Some partitions are not fully representable; the general situation is that, if the partition is in a represented form (with (
122
) holding), then the algebras for composite systems take the form

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{S}&=\Bigg\{\phi_{S}\,\Big(f_{A_{n},\,n\in S}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{A_{n},\,n\not\in S}\Big)\,\phi_{S}^{\dagger}\,\,\tilde{\mathbb{\Pi}}\\
&\qquad\Bigg|\,\,f\in\Lin_{\eta_{S}}\left(\bigotimes_{n\in S}\mathcal{H}_{A_{n}}\right)\Bigg\}\\
&=\hat{\phi}_{S}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S}\right)\,,\end{split}

(124)

where
\phi_{S}
is a dephasing unitary, belonging to
\textrm{Unit}\left(\bigvee_{x\in X}\mathcal{Z}_{x}\right)
, and
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S}
denotes the ‘well-localised’ form (
123
) of the algebra. When dealing with a non-fully representable partition, it is not possible to find a representation in which all of the
\phi_{S}
’s jointly disappear.

Fortunately, we have the following result.

Theorem F.1
.

All the partitions at hand in Theorem
D.2
are fully representable.

Since the construction of Theorem
D.2
is based on successive node-splittings using Lemma
D.9
, Theorem
F.1
is a direct consequence of the following Lemma.

Lemma F.3
.

In Lemma
D.9
, if
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}}
is fully representable, then
(\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{split}}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma^{\textrm{split}}}
is fully representable.

Before we prove this lemma, let us display three useful results. In each of them, we consider the partition
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq X}\vdash{{{\omega}}}
, in a represented form given by (
124
).

Lemma F.4
.

We fix
R\subseteq S\subseteq T\subseteq X
. If
\mathcal{A}_{R}
and
\mathcal{A}_{T}
are well-localised (i.e. of the form (
123
)), then

\phi_{S}\in\textrm{Unit}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{R}\vee\bigvee_{x\in T\setminus R}\mathcal{Z}_{x}\right)\,.

(125)

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{A}_{T}
is well-localised, the restricted partition
(\mathcal{A}_{X})_{{T^{\prime}}\subseteq T}\vdash\mathcal{A}_{T}
is itself in a represented form satisfying (
122
), so we can apply the formula (
124
) to it and obtain

\phi_{S}\in\textrm{Unit}\left(\bigvee_{x\in T}\mathcal{Z}_{x}\right)\,.

(126)

Furthermore, since
\mathcal{A}_{R}
is well-localised, the coarse-grained version of the partition in which we consider
\mathcal{A}_{R}
as an individual part without substructure is in a represented form as well, and applying the above result to it yields (
125
).
∎

Lemma F.5
.

We fix
S\subseteq X
and suppose
{{{\omega}}}
factor. If
\mathcal{A}_{S}
is well-localised, then so is
\mathcal{A}_{\bar{S}}
.

Proof.

Since
{{{\omega}}}
is a factor,
\mathcal{A}_{S}=\mathcal{A}_{\bar{S}}^{\prime}
, and from their form it is clear that
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S}=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\bar{S}}^{\prime}
, so
\mathcal{A}_{S}=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{S}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{A}_{\bar{S}}=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\bar{S}}
.
∎

Lemma F.6
.

We fix
S,T\subseteq X
such that
\mathcal{A}_{S\cap T}=\mathcal{A}_{S}\cap\mathcal{A}_{T}
and suppose
{{{\omega}}}
factor. If
\mathcal{A}_{S}
and
\mathcal{A}_{T}
are well-localised, then so is
\mathcal{A}_{S\cap T}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
F.5
,
\mathcal{A}_{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}_{\bar{S}}
and
\mathcal{A}_{T}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}_{\bar{T}}
are well-localised, and thus so is their span
\mathcal{A}_{S}^{\prime}\vee\mathcal{A}_{T}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{A}_{S}\cap\mathcal{A}_{T})^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}_{S\cap T}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}_{\overline{S\cap T}}
; so by Lemma
F.5
again,
\mathcal{A}_{S\cap T}
is well-localised.
∎

We now prove Lemma
F.3
.

Proof.

Up to an isomorphism, we can assume without loss of generality that
(\mathcal{A}_{S}^{\textrm{split}})_{S\subseteq\Gamma^{\textrm{split}}}
is in its represented form (
124
). Since its coarse-graining
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}}
is fully representable, we can pick a representation in which, for every
S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}\setminus\{0\}
,
\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{split}}_{S}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{split}}_{S\sqcup\{-\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{4}\}}
are well-localised, i.e.
\phi_{S}
and
\phi_{S\sqcup\{-\frac{1}{4},\frac{1}{4}\}}
are identities.

In particular,
\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{split}}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\textrm{split}}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-1\rrbracket}
are then well-localised; so, by Lemma
F.4
applied with
R=\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-1\rrbracket\subseteq S=\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket\subseteq T=\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket
, we have

\begin{split}\phi_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\in\,\,&\textrm{Unit}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-1\rrbracket}\vee\mathcal{Z}_{-\frac{1}{4}}\vee\mathcal{Z}_{\frac{1}{4}}\right)\\
&\subseteq\textrm{Unit}\left((\mathcal{Z}_{{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{p_{\shortm}})\vee\mathcal{A}_{0}\right)\,,\end{split}

(127)

where
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}=\hat{\phi}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket})
, with
\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
the localised algebra of the form (
123
).

Let us prove that
\phi_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\in\textrm{Unit}(\mathcal{A}_{0})
. Denoting
\mathcal{Z}_{{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{p_{\shortm}}
’s atomic projectors as
\pi^{k}
’s, we have
\phi_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}=\sum_{k}\pi^{k}\phi^{k}
, with the
\phi^{k}
’s in
\textrm{Unit}(\mathcal{A}_{0})
.

We then take
f\in\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
; since it is in
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
,
f=\hat{\phi}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}(\tilde{f})
with
\tilde{f}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
. Furthermore,

\begin{split}\tilde{f}&\in\hat{\phi}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket})\\
&\subseteq\hat{\phi}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\dagger}(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket})\\
&\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\\
&=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},{q_{\shortm}}-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},{q_{\shortm}}-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}\,;\end{split}

(128)

therefore
\tilde{f}\in\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\cap\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},{q_{\shortm}}-1\rrbracket}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{Z}_{{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{p_{\shortm}})\vee\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
.

We can thus write it as
\tilde{f}=\sum_{k}\pi^{k}\tilde{f}^{k}
, with the
\tilde{f}^{k}
’s in
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
. This yields
f=\sum_{k}\pi^{k}\hat{\phi}^{k}(\tilde{f}^{k})
. Picking an arbitrary
k_{0}
, we then have
\pi^{k_{0}}f=\pi^{k_{0}}\hat{\phi}^{k_{0}}(\tilde{f}^{k_{0}})
, where
f
and
\hat{\phi}^{k_{0}}(\tilde{f}^{k_{0}})
are in
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},0\rrbracket}
. However, multiplication by
\pi^{k_{0}}
is injective on the latter, since Lemma
D.6
tells us that the
\mu
corresponding to it has to be the identity by correlation length, so
f=\hat{\phi}^{k_{0}}(\tilde{f}^{k_{0}})
.

Therefore,
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\subseteq\hat{\phi}^{k_{0}}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket})\subseteq\hat{\phi}^{k_{0}}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket})
with
\phi^{k_{0}}\in\textrm{Unit}(\mathcal{A}_{0})
. We also note that, since
\phi^{k_{0}}
commutes with all elements of
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-1\rrbracket}=\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-1\rrbracket}\subseteq\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
, we have
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-1\rrbracket}=\hat{\phi}^{k_{0}}(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-1\rrbracket})\subseteq\hat{\phi}^{k_{0}}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket})
. Thus,

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}&=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\\
&\overset{(\ref{eq: restric qmin})}{=}(\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime})^{\prime}\\
&=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket 0,{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}^{\prime}\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\\
&=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-1\rrbracket}\vee\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{q_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}\\
&\subseteq\hat{\phi}^{k_{0}}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket})\,,\end{split}

(129)

which we can turn into an equality
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}=\hat{\phi}^{k_{0}}(\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket})
since
\hat{\phi}^{k_{0}}
is bijective. This shows that
\phi_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
is in
\textrm{Unit}(\mathcal{A}_{0})
.

We move to a new representation by conjugating
{{{\omega}}}
by
\phi_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\dagger}
. Because it is in
\textrm{Unit}(\mathcal{A}_{0})
, this does not modify the representation of the
\mathcal{A}_{S}
and
\mathcal{A}_{S\sqcup\{-\frac{1}{4},+\frac{1}{4}\}}
’s for
S\subseteq\Gamma_{N}\setminus\{0\}
; indeed
\phi_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\dagger}
commutes past the first, and can be absorbed in the second as they include
\mathcal{A}_{0}
. Thus, this yields a representation in which these as well as
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
are localised. Symmetrically,
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}^{\prime}
is localised as well by Lemma
F.5
. Furthermore, for
n\in\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket
,
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n,-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}=\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket n,0\rrbracket}\cap\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket{p_{\shortm}},-\frac{1}{4}\rrbracket}
is the intersection of two localised algebras and is thus localised by Lemma
F.6
, and the same goes symmetrically for the
\mathcal{A}_{\llbracket\frac{1}{4},n\rrbracket}
’s with
n\in\llbracket\frac{1}{4},{p_{\scalebox{.5}{\textrm{+}}}}\rrbracket
. One can find that the same is true for larger intervals by taking commutants (using Lemma
F.5
), and finally for non-intervals by taking spans.

∎

F.3
Representations of strongly connected 1D partitions

Representations of partitions — i.e. how a partitioned algebra can be seen as a space of operators over a suitably partitioned Hilbert space, in such a way that each of the partitions’ subalgebras becomes the subalgebra of operators acting non-trivially only on the corresponding part of the Hilbert space — were studied in the general case in Ref.
[
21
]
. We will work directly with this paper’s notations, and assume knowledge of the notions it introduces.

The representation of a partition involves the use of Boolean matrices, pinning down sectorial correlations and routes, which in the general case can be quite convoluted. What we will now show is that, in the specific case of strongly connected 1D partitions (on a graph of size strictly greater than three times their correlation length), one can present these routes in a more accessible way, that only involves Kronecker deltas between neighbouring sites, as introduced in the main text.

For the remainder of this section, we take
(\mathcal{A}_{S})_{S\subseteq X}\vdash{{{\omega}}}
to be a strongly connected 1D partition of a factor, with correlation length at most
l
, where
N\geq 3l+1
.

Since for every
n\in X
,
\mathcal{Z}_{n}=(\mathcal{Z}_{n-1}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{n})\vee(\mathcal{Z}_{n}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{n+1})
, the idea is to label the former algebra’s set of atomic projectors with a pair of labels, corresponding to atomic projectors of the latter two algebras. We will thus label these latter atomic projectors in the following way:

\begin{split}\forall n\in\Gamma_{N}^{+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}},\quad&\textrm{AtomProj}(\mathcal{Z}_{n-\frac{1}{2}}\cap\mathcal{Z}_{n+\frac{1}{2}})\\
&=\left\{\pi_{n}^{k_{n}}\,\,\middle|\,\,k_{n}\in K_{n}\right\}\,.\end{split}

(130)

For a given
n\in\Gamma_{N}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
’s atomic projectors are then almost the
\pi_{n-\frac{1}{2}}^{k_{n}^{\textrm{L}}}\,\,\pi_{n+\frac{1}{2}}^{k_{n}^{\textrm{R}}}
where
k_{n}^{\textrm{L}}\in K_{n-\frac{1}{2}}
and
k_{n}^{\textrm{R}}\in K_{n+\frac{1}{2}}
, with the difference that the latter might also, in addition, include one or several instances of the null projector
0
. We thus need the following proposition.

Proposition F.1

(Null-sector trick)
.

The construction of a partitioned algebra’s routes displayed in Ref.
[
21
]
also works if, for every
n
, we allow some of the
\pi_{n}^{k_{n}}
’s to be null, as long as the
\pi_{n}^{k_{n}}
’s are still pairwise orthogonal projectors spanning
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
.

Proof.

The proofs in Ref.
[
21
]
never use the fact that the
\pi_{n}^{k_{n}}
’s are non-null.
∎
